# Translator's Note

## 1. The text-base

This working English translation follows the **posthumous Latin edition** of Kirchner's methodical systematic, printed at **Leipzig by Johann Beyer in the year M.D.XCV (1595)**, under the full title ***Methodica Explicatio Praecipuorum Capitum Doctrinae Coelestis, ita conscripta ut non solum brevem hypothesin veritatis Christianae contineat, verum etiam solidam refutationem pugnantium opinionum complectatur*** — *"A Methodical Exposition of the Chief Chapters of the Heavenly Doctrine, so composed as to comprise not only a brief hypothesis of Christian truth, but also a solid refutation of the opinions that oppose it."*

The title-page adds a **crucial second sub-title:** ***"CVI ADDITA EST VITA ET curriculum ministerii eiusdem D. Timothei Kirchneri piè defuncti: Et in locis singulis ad marginem notatio, ex quibus D. Lutheri scriptis, in quibus plenius explicantur, ij sint desumpti: locorum item Scripturae toto hoc libro explicatorum, et capitum totius libri . . . index"* — *"To which is added the Life and Course of the Ministry of the same D. Timotheus Kirchner, piously deceased; and at each single Locus a marginal notation, showing the places in D. Luther's writings from which they are drawn; likewise an Index of the Scripture-passages here expounded, and of the Chapters of the whole book."***

The source-text used here is the Google Books digitisation of a library copy (48.6 MB PDF), from which we have extracted the per-page OCR text via **pdftotext (Poppler)** — yielding 51,945 lines of running late-humanist Latin. The OCR has the usual imperfections of Google-Books-quality 16th-century Latin (long-*s* → *f* / *ss* confusion, Greek-letter fallouts, marginal-note interpolations into the main text, dropped diacritics), which we correct silently against the underlying source-image.

## 2. What this book actually is

Kirchner himself, in the preface *ad Christiano Lectorem*, tells the story of the book's origin: *"Three years ago I published my German Enchiridion, and dedicated it to Elector Palatine Ludwig VI, at his own instigation. Since the publication of that book pleased many pious lovers of truth, they urged me to attempt something similar in the Latin tongue. Though I would gladly have satisfied their desire long ago, from the time I was compelled to leave the Palatinate so many most difficult and heavy hindrances called me away from this labour that, contrary to my expectation, I was not able to put my hand to it. At last, seeing that on account of other far graver occupations I could not perform what I wished and willed, I assigned it to my beloved son-in-law **Master Johann Fladung**, at that time pastor and superintendent of the church of Saalfeld, that in his spare hours he should convert the said Enchiridion into the Latin tongue. This labour he willingly undertook, and in a short space of time, by God's grace, brought it to a happy completion. On rereading his version and attending more carefully to each single point, it seemed good to me not only to alter certain things which stood in the German exemplar and had been already converted, but also to add other matters not to be despised to the earlier ones, so that the explication of the matters in this language might be somewhat fuller. It also came into my mind that I should be doing a not unwelcome service to the studious minds of piety if I added to each single matter the principal ***objections of the Adversaries with their solutions***, whereby the wits might be exercised and the judgment of the learners in matters of religion might be formed. By this reason it happened that the book grew not only in quantity, but was also rendered almost a new work; a thing which the reader will easily perceive from a collation of the German Enchiridion with this little book."*

The reader must therefore be told plainly: **this Methodica is not a mechanical Latin translation of the 1583 or 1592 Enchiridion.** It is:
- Kirchner's own **Latin-language remake**, based on Fladung's initial translation, which Kirchner then revised;
- **expanded** at many points where the Latin explication is fuller than the German original;
- **carrying at each Locus a formal apparatus of Objections + Solutions** which the German edition did not have;
- and (Fladung's second contribution, added in the son-in-law's own preface *ad Lectorem*) **anchored by marginal cross-references to specific volumes and pages of Luther's Jena and Wittenberg tomes** — turning the whole book into an indexed Luther-anthology arranged by systematic locus.

Kirchner died on 21 September 1587 (see the elegiac funeral poem by **Nicolaus Selnecker**, printed in the front-matter of the 1595 edition); the book therefore appeared eight years posthumously, seen through the press by Fladung.

## 3. Editorial conventions

**Locus-numbering.** Kirchner's 46 *Loci* are rendered as **Loci I–XLVI** in Roman numerals, following his own Index at the end of the volume. Where our English *Enchiridion* rendered the German *Hauptstücke* as *Chapters*, this Latin edition uses **Locus** throughout — matching the Latin *loci-communes* tradition of Melanchthon, Chemnitz, Selnecker, and Gerhard which is Kirchner's own idiom in this Latin work.

**Question format.** Every *Quaestio* is rendered as a numbered `Question` heading; the bolded restatement of the question precedes the answer, following the same convention as our Enchiridion volume.

**Objections apparatus.** Kirchner's ***OBIECTIONES*** — the formal apparatus of adversary syllogisms with responses that he added to the Latin edition — are rendered as numbered `Objection` sub-headings at the end of each Locus, following the exact syllogistic form of the source (major, minor, ergo — then ***Respondeo***).

**Luther marginalia.** Fladung's marginal cross-references to Luther (e.g. *T. 1 p. 80*, *T. 3 p. 68*, *Postilla Domestica Jena*) are preserved as **inline citations in italics within square brackets** at the point where they appear in the source margin, so that the reader can follow the Luther-anchor without leaving the page. Fladung's own note on the editions he consulted (Jena Latin tomes 1–4, Jena German tomes 1–8, Wittenberg Postilla Ecclesiastica 1562, Domestica Jena 1579) is preserved in his preface *ad Lectorem*.

**Latin and Greek.** The Latin of the *Methodica* is preserved *verbatim* in italics wherever Kirchner is quoting a Father, a Council, a syllogistic proposition, or a technical scholastic distinction (***sola Scriptura***, ***causa efficiens***, ***per verbum***, ***synergia***, ***libertas christiana***, ***iudicium contradictionis***); each is followed by a plain-English gloss on first occurrence. Greek words retained by Kirchner in Roman characters (***Scheol***, ***Gehenna***, ***catechumenus***) are preserved without transliteration.

**Bible citations.** Book abbreviations are normalised to the modern English standard (Rom., 1 Cor., Matt.); chapter and verse numbers are silently corrected against the underlying reference where Kirchner's citation differs from modern versification. The Latin *verf.* (verse) is expanded to *v.* in English.

**Bold and italics.** **Bold** marks Bible citations, Kirchner's enumerated *primo / secundo / tertio* points, and the load-bearing thesis in each answer. *Italics* mark Latin quotations, Greek transliterations, and technical scholastic vocabulary retained in the original tongue. Section dividers use the aldus-leaf ornament — ❦ —.

**Polemical labels.** As in our Enchiridion volume, Kirchner's polemical distinctions — ***Pontificii***, ***Sacramentarii***, ***Anabaptistae***, ***Novatiani***, ***Chiliastae***, ***Sadducaei***, ***Enthusiastae***, ***Ebionitae***, ***Marcionitae*** — are preserved as he uses them, and rendered by the natural English equivalents (**Papists**, **Sacramentarians**, etc.).

**Silent OCR corrections.** Long-*s* → *f* and *ss* → *β* confusions, dropped Greek diacritics, marginal-note text interpolated into the main body by the OCR flow, and line-break hyphen artefacts from Poppler's extraction are silently corrected against the source-image. Substantive editorial decisions that touch the doctrine are noted in the endnotes.

## 4. Reader navigation

The book contains **46 Loci** across the whole span of Christian doctrine, following the same order as the German *Enchiridion*: from **Sacred Scripture** (Locus I) and the **Doctrine of God** through the person and offices of **Christ**, the **Holy Spirit**, **Creation**, the **Fall**, **Sin**, **Law and Gospel**, **Justification**, the **Sacraments**, the **Church**, the **Antichrist**, and the **Last Things** — closing with **Eternal Life** (Locus XLVI). A full **Table of Contents** follows this Note, grouped into six parts. Each Locus opens with a divider ornament and closes with the same before the next Locus begins; the formal **Objections** appear at the end of each Locus in Roman numerals. The **Endnotes** appear as a numbered section following Locus XLVI. This volume is a **companion** to the Scholastic Lutherans edition of the German *Enchiridion* (1592) — the two together give Kirchner in both his tongues, with the Latin adding a formal *disputationes*-style apparatus and a page-anchored Luther-index that the German lacks.

# Locus I. Of Sacred Scripture, or the Word of God

### *De Sacra Scriptura seu Verbo Dei*

— ❦ —

## Question 1. From what foundations is it proved that the Christian religion and faith alone is the true religion and faith?

**From what foundations is it demonstrated that the religion and faith of Christians alone is the true religion and faith?**

**I. From antiquity.** For no religion on this earth, of whatever name, has such authority as the Christian religion attains by antiquity and duration. This faith **Adam** and all the **Patriarchs** embraced; and this same faith **all** who shall be heirs of blessedness will retain **until the end of the world**.

**Gen. 3:15** — *"I will put enmities between thee and the woman, and between thy seed and her seed; she [or: he] shall crush thy head, and thou shalt lie in wait for her heel."*

**Acts 10:43** — *"To him [Jesus] all the Prophets bear witness, that all who believe in him shall receive remission of sins through his name."*

**Rom. 4:3** — *"For what saith the Scripture? Abraham believed God, and it was reputed to him unto justice."*

**II.** **No religion in the world has been so confirmed and established by so many illustrious and truly divine works and miracles as the Christian faith** — as the histories of the Old and New Testaments abundantly witness. Besides this, no religion, though it has attempted them, has been able to bring forth similar works.

**III.** **No religion has been sealed with the blood of so many true martyrs** — who suffered for truth and righteousness. None has endured and conquered through so many perils and persecutions except the Christian. For though from the beginning of the world many have been slain for the sake of this religion, the religion has not been *cut off*; and though it has been assaulted by all other sects, it has never been *conquered*.

**IV.** **No religion besides the Christian has sure predictions and prophecies concerning things to come.** For whatsoever concerning its own future and that of other sects, whatsoever concerning **Christ**, the **Church**, and the political mutations of empires has been foretold in Sacred Scripture many ages back, the outcome has confirmed and daily confirms. To all the oracles and predictions of the sacred letters the deeds and the events have answered, and answer still. **Isa. 43:11–12** — *"I am, I am the Lord, and there is no Saviour besides me: I have announced, and I have saved,"* etc.

**V.** **Above all**, the doctrine of the **Gospel** — proper to Christians, unknown to men of all other sects — proves and demonstrates the truth and certitude of the Christian religion. For without this Gospel-doctrine, whoever live in the world are ***without God***, and ***have no hope*** (**Eph. 2:12**). And this Gospel-doctrine, they who embrace it by true faith, by the witness of the experience of all the faithful, have **solid, immovable, and efficacious consolation, tranquility, and peace of heart in all temptations and afflictions**; whence they easily come to rest, and gather that they are accepted to God in Christ. **Rom. 5:1–2** — *"Being therefore justified by faith, let us have peace with God, through our Lord Jesus Christ, by whom also we have access through faith into this grace, wherein we stand, and glory in the hope of the glory of the sons of God."*

— ❦ —

## Question 2. What then is the Word of God, or Sacred Scripture?

**What then is the Word of God, or Sacred Scripture?**

**It is a doctrine given by God himself, in which — by the ministry of Law and Gospel — we are instructed concerning God, both as to his Essence and as to his Will toward us men, and are sanctified unto eternal life.** [*Luther: T. 1, p. 80; T. 5, p. 471; T. 5, p. 314*]

**John 17:17** — *"Sanctify them in truth; thy word is truth."*

— ❦ —

## Question 3. What are the chief chapters of Sacred Scripture, or of the Word?

**What are the chief chapters of Sacred Scripture?**

**Two: the Law and the Gospel.** [*Luther: T. 3, p. 165; T. 3, p. 68*]

The **Law** teaches and prescribes what we ought to do and to omit.

The **Gospel**, however, is not concerned with things to be done and omitted, but **freely offers the remission of sins and reconciliation with God, on account of Christ**. **John 3:16** — *"So God loved the world, that he gave his only-begotten Son, that whosoever believeth in him should not perish, but have life everlasting."*

— ❦ —

## Question 4. Whence does Sacred Scripture take its origin?

**Whence does Sacred Scripture take its origin?**

**From God himself.** For at **Exod. 32:15–16** we read: *"And Moses returned from the mount, carrying the two tables of the testimony in his hand, written on both sides, and made by the work of the Lord; the writing also of God was graven in the tables."*

Whence it is clear that God himself first ***consigned his word and revelations to letters***, from which also we understand the dignity and majesty of the Sacred Letters. Moses hereafter, by God's command (as we read at **Exod. 34**), consigned the whole Law to letters and left it to posterity; and him the Prophets, Evangelists, and Apostles have followed.

— ❦ —

## Question 5. By whom was the Word of God first made known?

**By whom was the Word of God first made known?**

**By God himself.** [*Luther: T. 8, p. 134; Genesis T. 3, p. 1; Genesis T. 4, pp. 119, 120*] As **Ps. 103:7** says: *"He made known his ways to Moses, his wills to the children of Israel."* And **2 Tim. 3:16** — *"All Scripture divinely inspired is profitable to teach, to reprove, to correct, to instruct in justice, that the man of God may be perfect, furnished to every good work."*

— ❦ —

## Question 6. What moved God to this self-manifestation?

**What moved God to this self-manifestation?**

**Nothing other than his inexhaustible and ineffable mercy**, on account of which he willed to make himself known to us by a sure and certain Word — especially since men, having fallen into sin, had lost the true knowledge of God (**Gen. 3**), and he willed not to leave them in those shadows and in ignorance of his essence and of his will; for otherwise all men would have had to perish eternally in those shadows.

— ❦ —

## Question 7. Where and among whom may this Word of God be found?

**Where and among whom may this Word of God be found?**

**Only in the true Church of God**, to which God himself has made himself known by his Word and by illustrious miracles. [*Luther: T. 7, p. 93; Genesis T. 3, p. ; T. 3, p. 303*]

**Deut. 4:6** — *"This is your wisdom, and understanding in the sight of peoples: that hearing all these precepts, they may say — Behold a wise and understanding people, a great nation. Nor is there any other nation so great, that hath gods so nigh them, as our God is present to all our petitions. For what other nation is there so renowned, that hath ceremonies and just judgements, and all the law which I set forth this day before your eyes."*

And **Ps. 147:19–20** — *"Who declareth his word to Jacob, his justices and his judgements to Israel. He hath not done in like manner to every nation, and his judgements he hath not made manifest to them."*

**John 10:27, 5** — *"My sheep hear my voice; but a stranger they follow not, but flee from him, because they know not the voice of strangers."*

— ❦ —

## Question 8. What must a Christian man chiefly seek in Scripture? And what is the chief scope of Sacred Scripture?

**What must a Christian man chiefly seek in Scripture, and what is the chief scope of the Sacred Scripture?**

**In Sacred Scripture, Christ Jesus, the Saviour of the world, is above all to be sought — with his merit and benefits.** [*Luther: T. 7, p. 187; T. 8, p. 258*] For to him the whole Scripture points us; to him the whole Scripture bears witness. **John 5:39** — *"Search ye the Scriptures; they are they that give testimony of me."*

— ❦ —

## Question 9. What are the chief properties of Sacred Scripture?

**What are the chief properties of Sacred Scripture?**

**These:**

**I.** That Sacred Scripture is the **Lydian stone**, the **certain rule**, and the **infallible canon of all human writings** — apart from which, unless they be approved by it, they are nothing. [*Luther: T. 3, p. 165; T. 1, p. 402; T. 4, p. 20*]

**Ps. 19:8** — *"The testimony of the Lord is faithful, giving wisdom to little ones."*

Whence **Augustine, *Contra Epistolam Petiliani***: ***"Nec catholicis Episcopis consentiendum est, sicubi forte falluntur, aut contra canonicas Dei Scripturas aliquid sentiunt."*** — *"Neither with the Catholic Bishops themselves is one to consent, wherever they perhaps are deceived, or teach anything contrary to the canonical Scriptures of God."*

**II.** That Scripture is **more sure and firm than all councils**. For the councils themselves must be instituted according to Scripture, and depend upon it. [*Luther: T. 7, p. 255*]

**Gal. 1:8** — *"But though we, or an angel from heaven, preach a gospel to you besides that which we have preached to you, let him be anathema."*

Whence **Jerome**: ***"Spiritus Sancti doctrina est, quae canonicis literis est prodita; contra quam si quid statuant concilia, nefas duco."*** — *"It is the doctrine of the Holy Spirit which is set forth in the canonical letters; against which if the councils establish anything, I count it profane."*

**III.** Scripture ***admits no syncretism or human mixture***; it wills to be either alone or nothing. [*Luther: T. 1, p. 503, f. b.; T. 5, p. 391; T. 3, part 1, p. 120*]

Whence **Ps. 12:7** — *"The silver examined by fire, proved, and purged sevenfold."*

**IV.** Scripture is of ***unsearchable and inexhaustible wisdom***. [*Luther: T. 5, p. 440; T. 5, p. 125*] As **Ps. 147:5** — *"Great is our Lord, and great is his power; and of his wisdom there is no number."*

Whensoever any Christian therefore, of whatever condition or dignity, ought to search it and turn it over day and night by hand. Whence **Chrysostom, *Colossians* 3**, says: *"Hear, I entreat you, all secular ones: procure for yourselves Bibles, the pharmacies of the soul; or if nothing else, then at least acquire the New Testament, the Acts of the Apostles, the Gospels — as continual and diligent teachers. If molestation shall happen, look into these as into a pharmacy: from here take for yourself consolation, whether some damage happen, or death, or loss of household. Yea, not only look in, but pass all over again and again, and hold them in mind. For hence is the cause of all evils, that the Scriptures are ignored,"* etc.

— ❦ —

## Question 10. Whence, therefore, is it clear that Sacred Scripture is a doctrine certain and firm, in which we may and must safely trust?

**Whence is it clear that Sacred Scripture is a certain and firm doctrine, on which we may and must safely rest?**

**From these:** [*Luther: T. 4, p. 120, f. b.*]

**I.** That it was first ***consigned to letters by God himself***.

**II.** That it is ***divinely inspired*** — **2 Tim. 3:16**.

**III.** That from the beginning it was ***confirmed by so many illustrious and truly divine miracles***.

**IV.** That the ***holy men of God who wrote it were impelled by God himself*** — **2 Pet. 1:19**: *"And we have the more firm prophetical word, whereunto you do well to attend, as to a light that shineth in a dark place,"* etc.

From which it is evident that Scripture is by far to be preferred to the writings of the Heathen.

— ❦ —

## Objections

### Objection I. The most ancient religion of all is most to be believed. The religion of the Heathen is the most ancient of all. *Ergo*, it is most to be believed.

**Respondeo**: The **minor is manifestly false**. For the doctrine of the Church alone is the most ancient — inasmuch as God himself, in Paradise, immediately after the creation and the fall of the first parents, made it known (**Gen. 3**). Neither is there any more ancient history in the world which sets forth the creation of all things than the history of **Moses**. And collation shows that Moses and the Prophets are more ancient than the Greeks; for **Herodotus** begins there where the Prophet **Jeremiah** ends, as at **Jer. 44** it is permitted to see and to read. Whence **Justin Martyr, in his *Apology* to Antoninus**: ***"Moses is more ancient than all the Greek writers."*** And **Africanus** in **Eusebius, *De Praeparatione Evangelica* lib. 10**: ***"Whatever among the Greeks excels in the praise of antiquity, all that follows Moses by a long interval."***

### Objection II. The universal doctrine of virtue is comprehended in the Attic and Roman laws and in the writings of the philosophers. *Ergo*, the Heathen religion is true, and to be received by all.

**Respondeo**: The **antecedent is most false**. For the Attic and Roman laws and the writings of the philosophers scarcely contain a *shadow* of the doctrine of the virtues. [*Postilla domestica, T. 2*] For first, of the true recognition and invocation of God they teach nothing. Of the true fear of God, of the love of God, of faith in God, and of hope — they have **not even a word**.

Next, though they contain certain things concerning the virtues of the **Second Table**, all is nevertheless *maimed and imperfect* if compared with Sacred Scripture. Add to this that they are utterly ignorant of the doctrine of sin and of the redemption of the human race through Christ, of the free remission of sins, and the like.

### Objection III. Does not the Roman Pope rightly say, that Scripture has its authority from the Church?

**Minime** — By no means. For Scripture has its authority ***only from God***. But that the Roman Pontiff refers the authority of Scripture back to the Church, is *just as if you were to prefer the daughter to the mother, and the wife to the husband*. For Scripture is as it were the **mother** from whom the Church is begotten; and as it were the **husband** whose wife the Church is — and not the contrary.

**But (they urge) the Church was before the Word of God was consigned to letters?**

Although there were faithful men before the Word of God was consigned to letters, it matters nothing. For the Word of God was **prior to the faithful themselves** — although it was not yet committed to letters. The first parents did not indeed have the Word *written*, but they had nevertheless a **certain Word of God** through which they were called to the fellowship of faith and of the Church, as at **Gen. 3:4** is to be seen. For unless God, through his Word, had made himself known to them chiefly after the fall, they would have known nothing certain of him, nor been living members of the Church. *For it is through the Word that we believe* — **John 17**.

### Objection IV. Do all things which are necessary to salvation, then, contain and are found in Scripture?

**The Papists deny this — with the greatest injury and contumely to Scripture — but wrongly.** For at **2 Tim. 3:16–17** it is expressly written: *"All Scripture divinely inspired is profitable, that the man of God may be perfect, furnished unto every good work."* Whence also **Christ, Luke 16:29**, says: *"They have Moses and the Prophets: let them hear them."*

Although the New Testament was at that time not yet written, yet **nothing has been written in it afterward which does not have its own immoveable foundation in the Old Testament** — as is clear from the harmony and consent of the Old and New Testaments.

And at **John 20:30–31** the Evangelist says: *"Many other signs also did Jesus in the sight of his disciples, which are not written in this book; but these are written, that you may believe that Jesus is the Christ, the Son of God, and that believing you may have life in his name."*

Hither belongs the voice of **Cyril on John**: *"Not all things which the Lord did are written, but what the writers thought sufficient both for morals and for dogmas."* [*Cyril: on the sufficiency of Scripture*]

And **Augustine, *In Ioannem*, Tractate 49**: *"That Christ did many things which are not written, but that they were chosen which should be written, which seemed to suffice for the salvation of believers."*

Whence also **Athanasius, *Contra Gentes***, writes: ***"Sufficere Scripturas divinitus inspiratas ad omnem instructionem veritatis."*** — *"That the Scriptures divinely inspired are sufficient for every instruction of the truth."*

### Objection V. If Sacred Scripture were sufficient, there would be no need of Apostolic traditions, of the decrees of councils, of the writings of the Fathers, and of the declaration of the Catholic Church. But there is need of traditions, etc. *Ergo*, Sacred Scripture is not sufficient.

**Respondeo**: The **minor is false** — namely, that Scripture should be completed out of traditions, out of the decrees of councils, out of the writings of the Fathers, and out of the Church's declaration.

For as concerns the ***traditions*** which the Papists peddle under the name of the Apostles: **first**, it is agreed that they are *uncertain*, and cannot with firm reasons be shown to have been left by the Apostles. **Second**, it is manifest that the traditions the Papists press very often *conflict with the writings of the Apostles themselves*. **Third**, whatsoever they be, they are to be examined by the *norm of Scripture*, and where they conflict with it, simply rejected. For at **Col. 2:8** the Apostle bids us take heed that we be not led astray by vain deception, according to the tradition of men. **Fourth**, it is in itself most unworthy to feign that human traditions are the *completion* of the divinely inspired Scripture. **Fifth**, from **Irenaeus** and others it is agreed that of old the heretics also turned to the accusation of the Scriptures, and, as though they were not sufficient, appealed to traditions. And **Tertullian *Against Hermogenes*** says: ***"Scriptum esse doceat Hermogenis officina: si non est scriptum, timeat vae illud adijcientibus aut detrahentibus, destinatum."*** — *"Let the workshop of Hermogenes teach that it is written; if it is not written, let him fear that woe which is destined for those who add or take away."*

As concerns the ***councils***: it is certain that they are to be heard no further than while they confirm their decrees out of the ***fountains of Israel***, or out of the Sacred Letters. Whence **Jerome to the Galatians** wrote: ***"Spiritus Sancti doctrina est, quae canonicis literis prodita est; contra quam si quid statuant concilia, nefas duco."*** — *"It is the doctrine of the Holy Spirit which is set forth in the canonical letters; against which if the councils establish anything, I count it profane."* [*See: doctrine of councils; is it to be received?*] For if we ought to believe the councils apart from Scripture, in vain would Scripture be called ***Canonical***.

And **Chrysostom, Homily 13 on 2 Corinthians**, says: *"Since we have the most exact balance, the gnomon and rule — the assertion of the divine laws — let us abandon what seems best to this or that man, and inquire all things from the Scriptures."*

In the same manner concerning the ***Fathers***, it is certain that with their writings they cannot complete and perfect Sacred Scripture. For there is a vast difference between ***Canonical letters*** and the writings of the Fathers. So **Augustine, *Epistle 48 to Vincentius***, writes: *"This kind of letters is to be distinguished from the authority of the Canon. For they are not so read as if testimony were brought forward from them, that one might not think otherwise, if perhaps they knew otherwise than the truth demands."* And ***Epistle 3 to Fortunatus***: *"Nor may we hold the disputations of any men whatsoever, however Catholic and praiseworthy, as the Canonical Scriptures — that it should not be lawful, saving that honour which is due to those men, to disapprove or reject anything in their writings, if perhaps we find that they thought otherwise than the truth requires, either by divine help understood by others, or by ourselves. Such am I in the writings of others; such do I wish the understanders of my own to be."*

Concerning the ***Church*** it is agreed that she is a Church only ***upon condition*** — namely, so long as she hears and follows the Word of God (**John 10:5, 27**). *"My sheep hear my voice, and follow not a stranger."* How, therefore, could Sacred Scripture be completed and perfected by her?

Great indeed is the authority of the Church, but she extends herself no further than as she follows the Word of God, or Sacred Scripture. Hence **Ambrose, *Upon Luke***: *"If there is any Church which rejects faith, and does not possess the foundations of the Apostolic preaching, she must be forsaken."* And **Augustine, *De Unitate Credendi*, ch. 2**: *"In the words of the Lord, who is the truth, the Church is to be sought."*

### Objection VI. But at **2 Thess. 2:15** the Apostle himself refers the Church back to traditions. *Ergo*, Sacred Scripture is completed by traditions.

**Respondeo**: **The Apostle speaks of those things which by living voice he had handed on to the Thessalonians before the books of the New Testament were written and published; which nevertheless were not different from, nor contrary to, those which he afterward committed to letters.** By no means, therefore, does he understand those traditions which they call *tacit* — which were not committed by the Apostles to letters — but by the word *traditions* he embraces that same doctrine which he afterward began to commit to letters. And of Scripture, at **2 Tim. 3**, which he wrote about the end of his life, when the books of the New Testament had already been published, he plainly affirms that ***it is sufficient for salvation, and renders the believing man perfect***.

### Objection VII. But **Irenaeus** and **Tertullian** appeal to traditions and to the most ancient Churches. *Ergo*, etc.

**Respondeo**: **They appeal to the most ancient and first Churches of the Apostles, and those which had been planted by the Apostles — not to the present Roman Church, such as it now is.** Next, it must be considered what kind of heretics they were against whom these Fathers pressed the tradition or authority of the Church — namely, of such a sort as *partly rejected the Scriptures, partly corrupted them*, as the Marcionites and the like.

— ❦ —

## Question 11. In the interpretation of Scripture, however, how can a Christian man know which interpretation is true and which false?

**In the interpretation of Scripture, how can a Christian man know which interpretation is true and which false?**

**Respondeo**: **Let him hold this infallible rule handed down by Paul at Rom. 12:6**: *"All prophecy or interpretation ought to be according to the ratio or analogy of faith."* [*Luther: T. 4, p. 2; T. 8, p. 258; T. 5, p. 137, 300; T. 7, p. 120; T. 8, p. 326; T. 3, p. 38, 65; T. 4, p. 377; T. 5, p. 62; T. 2, p. 322; T. 2, part 1, p. 2; Gen. T. 4, pp. 197, 201; T. 3, part 1, p. 158, f. b.; T. 4, part 1, p. 273; Gen. T. 1, p. 126; T. 2, part 1, p. 387; Gen. T. 1, p. 76; Gen. T. 3, p. 115; Gen. T. 1, p. 945*]

Those Doctors, therefore, who teach according to the *analogy of faith* and the *chief-heads of piety or of the Catechism*, are to be heard, and their doctrine received. Those, however, who teach not according to the analogy of faith — whether it be the Pope or another — we ought neither to hear nor to follow. For into Christ *alone*, that we may hear him — not into the Roman Pontiff, or the Doctors, or the Church — we are baptised.

The Rule of Paul, **Rom. 12**, therefore, cannot deceive us. Whence **Augustine, *Contra Faustum Manichaeum*, lib. 11**, pronounces of the books of all doctors: ***"Hoc genus literarum non cum credendi necessitate, sed cum iudicandi libertate legendum est."*** — *"This kind of letters is to be read not with the necessity of believing, but with the liberty of judging."*

— ❦ —

## Question 12. Is a Christian man, then, bound at once to believe and consent upon the mere allegation of one or another saying of Scripture?

**Is a Christian man bound at once to believe and consent upon a bare allegation of Scripture?**

**No.** But he is bound to *attend and to discern* whether the allegation be **entire and pertinent**. [*Luther: T. 1, p. 269; T. 3, p. 38*] For if anything is to be received from Scripture to demonstrate a matter, it is necessary that the Scripture be alleged ***entirely and to the point*** — otherwise even the **Devil** at **Matt. 4** and the heretics cite Scripture, but **falsely**.

But the most certain rule of proof is the ***Catechism***. [*The Catechism as the Lydian stone*] If the interpretation is according to the analogy and articles of faith and of the Catechism-heads, it is to be received; if not, it is to be rejected and shunned.

— ❦ —

## Question 13. What is the ***energeia*** — the force, efficacy, and operation of Scripture, or of the Word of God?

**What is the force, efficacy, and operation of Scripture, or of the Word of God?**

**I.** Through the Word the **Holy Spirit is given**, illuminating the hearts, and the ***remission of sins*** is offered. [*Luther: T. 6, p. 336; T. 3, p. 163; T. 7 f., p. 193; T. 4, p. 289 and 190; T. 6, p. 354; T. 5, p. 342*] **2 Cor. 3:6** — *"He made us fit to be ministers of the New Testament, not of the letter, but of the Spirit."* **Isa. 40:2** — *"Speak ye to the heart of Jerusalem, and call to her, for her malice is come to an end, her iniquity is forgiven: she hath received of the hand of the Lord double for all her sins."*

**II.** It renders **consciences peaceful and tranquil** — which human writings neither do nor can do. [*Luther: T. 4, p. 266; Gen. T. 3, p. 19*] **Eph. 4:11–14** — *"And he himself gave some indeed to be Apostles, etc., for the perfecting of the saints, in the work of the ministry, in the edification of the body of Christ: until we all meet unto the unity of faith and of the knowledge of the Son of God, into a perfect man, into the measure of the age of the fulness of Christ; that henceforth we be no more children tossed to and fro, and carried about with every wind of doctrine, in the wickedness of men, in cunning craftiness by which they lie in wait to deceive."*

**III.** It **puts the Devil to flight, aids, strengthens, and consoles against the world, the flesh, and all evil thoughts**. [*Luther: T. 4, p. 386*] Whence also **Psalm 1** calls those **blessed** who *meditate on the Word of God day and night*.

**IV.** It brings and bestows the **true blessedness of this life and of the future life** — and that abundantly, so that he who has the Word rejoices and glories even in extreme poverty and in the greatest afflictions, and loves this above many treasures of gold and silver, and does not exchange it for all the goods of the whole world. [*Luther: T. 5, p. 470*]

**V.** It **incorporates and engrafts us into Christ**, so that no creature — neither the Devil, nor the world, nor heretics — can separate us from Christ himself. [*Luther: T. 6, p. 192*]

And to embrace all together: ***Scripture divinely inspired is profitable to teach, to reprove, to correct, to instruct in justice, that the man of God may be perfect, furnished to every good work***. **2 Tim. 3:16–17**.

— ❦ —

## Question 14. Is it lawful for a Christian man, in a matter of faith or religion, to establish or institute anything **without a certain and express Word of God**?

**Is it lawful for a Christian man to establish anything in a matter of faith without an express Word of God?**

**By no means.** [*Luther: T. 8, p. 319; T. 4, p. 516; Gen. T. 2, p. 1916 A; Gen. T. 3, p. 75*] For at **1 Pet. 4:11**: *"If any man speak, let him speak as the words of God; if any man minister, let him do it as by the power which God administereth, that in all things God may be honoured through Jesus Christ,"* etc. — as though he said: *"Let all our sayings and doings have the solid foundation of the Word of God; where this is lacking to us, let us rest, and move nothing, lest we bring peril upon ourselves and upon others."*

— ❦ —

## Question 15. Do they act rightly who **extenuate or reject the Word of God, or the ministry of the Word, or the preaching of the Word**?

**Do they act rightly who extenuate or reject the Word of God, or the ministry of the Word, or the preaching of the Word?**

**By no means.** [*Luther: T. 6, pp. 199–200; T. 7, pp. 433–434; T. 6, p. 244; Gen. T. 2, p. 224; Gen. T. 4, p. 174; T. 4, part 1, p. 333–134*] For Christ himself at **John 17:20** says that ***we shall believe through the word of the Apostles, or through the preaching of the word***. That not all who hear the Word preached believe, the cause is *not in the Word*, but sticks in the *infidelity of the hearts*.

— ❦ —

## Question 16. Do the Papists say rightly that Scripture is ***obscure***?

**Do the Papists say rightly that Scripture is obscure?**

**No.** [*Luther: T. 1, p. 537, f. b.; T. 3, part 1, p. 163*] For **no other book was ever so equally perspicuous as the Bible**; and this book, compared to all others, is as ***the Sun among the stars***. And all the Fathers and ecclesiastical writers ***need to illustrate, ground, and demonstrate their books from this***. Whence **Ps. 119:105** compares Scripture to a **lamp**, as also **2 Pet. 1:19** — *"a lamp shining in a dark place, until the day dawn, and the Day-star arise in the hearts of men."* If, however, certain more obscure places or sayings occur in the Sacred Letters, they are to be declared from the more illustrious and more perspicuous.

### Objection VIII. The Fathers and interpreters everywhere confess that Scripture is obscure. *Ergo*, etc.

**Respondeo** with **Augustine, *De Doctrina Christiana***: [*Luther: T. 4, p. 377*] *"Although many things in the Scriptures are difficult and obscure, in those things which are manifestly set forth are found all things which pertain to faith and to the manners of living. Nothing indeed is drawn out from those obscurities which is not elsewhere spoken most plainly. That much remains hidden from many in the sacred letters, this happens not from the obscurity of Scripture, but from the blindness or negligence of those who do not so act as to see the clearest truth, but rather labour that all things may remain overwhelmed with darkness."* Of whom the Apostle **2 Cor. 4:3–4** writes: *"That the veil remains over their heart. And if our Gospel is hid, it is hid to those who are perishing, in whom the god of this age hath blinded the minds of unbelievers."* [*In Postilla domestica for Ash Wednesday, and the Sunday Sexagesima Gospel*]

### Objection IX. Peter at **2 Pet. 3:16** writes that the Epistles of Paul are ***difficult to understand***. *Ergo* they are obscure.

**Respondeo**: **First**, Peter does not say that *all* things in Paul's Epistles are obscure, but has regard to the question proposed concerning the ***Coming of the Lord*** — as the Greek words *ἐπὶ τούτοις* perspicuously show, which are to be referred to the neuter plural preceding — that is, to the question concerning the Coming of the Lord.

**Next**, he does not say that the reading of Paul's Epistles ***gives to the readers occasion of destruction***; but that ***unlearned and unstable men*** distort those things which he wrote of the Coming of the Lord, ***as also the rest of the Scriptures, to their own proper ruin***. Wherefore they act most wickedly who from these words accuse Scripture of obscurity.

— ❦ —

## Question 17. What must a Christian man do when the sayings of the Fathers are objected against Scripture?

**What must a Christian man do when the sayings of the Fathers are objected against Scripture?**

**Let him hold both this rule and the other.** [*Luther: T. 1, p. 538; T. 7, p. 243*]

**I.** Let him ask **whether the Fathers ever erred**. If they did err (as the adverse party, will he nill he, is compelled to confess), then ***their sayings by themselves demonstrate nothing***; but must be demonstrated from another firmer and per-se certain principle — that is, from a perspicuous testimony of Scripture. [*Luther: T. 3, part 1, p. 293, 297*] For **Sacred Scripture alone is the norm** according to which all dogmas and all cogitations are to be judged. **Ps. 19:8** — *"The testimony of the Lord is faithful, giving wisdom to little ones."* **Isa. 8:20** — *"To the Law rather, and to the Testimony; and if they speak not according to this word, they shall not have the morning light."*

**II.** Let him answer: **The Fathers do not wish to be believed apart from perspicuous and manifest testimonies of Scripture.** [*Augustine's own witness*] **Augustine to Jerome, Epistle 19**: *"For I confess to thy charity, that to those books of Scriptures alone which are now called Canonical I have learned to defer this honour and fear, that I most firmly believe no one of their authors to have erred in writing anything. But others I read in such wise that, with however great sanctity and learning they surpass, I do not therefore hold that thing to be true because they themselves have so thought; but because either through those Canonical authors, or by probable reason which does not depart from the truth, they have been able to persuade me. Nor do I think, my brother, that thou dost feel otherwise; namely, I in no wise consider that thou wouldest so wish thy books to be read as those of the Prophets or Apostles, of whose writings — that they lack all error — it is unlawful to doubt."*

— ❦ —

## Question 18. If, therefore, controversies arise in the cause of religion, who shall be the judge?

**If controversies arise in the cause of religion, who shall be the judge?**

**Let Sacred Scripture alone be the judge.** [*Luther: T. 4, p. 297*] For it is therefore called ***Canon*** — **Ps. 19:8–9; Gal. 6:16** — *"And whosoever shall follow this rule, peace on them, and mercy, and upon the Israel of God."* **Phil. 3:16** — *"Nevertheless whereunto we are come, that we be of the same mind, let us also continue in the same rule."*

Whence **Augustine, *De peccatorum meritis et remissione*, lib. 1, cap. 22**: [*Augustine's judgment*] *"Let us believe the Scripture, which neither can err, nor lead into error."*

And **Basil, Epistle 80**: [*Basil's judgment*] *"Let us stand at the arbitration of the Scriptures divinely inspired; and with whom are found dogmas consonant with the divine oracles, on those without doubt the verdict of truth falls."*

And **Constantine to the Fathers of the Council of Nicaea**: [*Constantine's judgment*] *"There are the Evangelical Books, and the Apostolic, and the sanctions of the ancient Prophets, which instruct us what we ought to think of the sacred Law. Casting off, therefore, the hostile contention, from the words divinely inspired let us receive the solution of the questions."*

And again **Augustine, *De nuptiis et concupiscentia***: [*Valerius's judgment*] *"This controversy seeks a judge. Let Christ therefore judge; let the Apostle judge with him — for Christ speaks in the Apostle."*

### Objection X. The matter of a lawsuit cannot be the supreme judge in causes of faith. Scripture is the matter of the suit. *Ergo* it cannot be the supreme judge.

**Respondeo**: **It is the fallacy of accident.** [*Luther: T. 7, p. 127*] For Scripture ***by itself*** is not matter of the suit, but ***by accident***. The sayings of Scripture concerning the articles of faith are ***per se*** plain and perspicuous; but by contentious men, whose hearts Satan has bewitched, they are distracted, perverted, obscured, and called into doubt. [*Luther: T. 4, part 1, p. 341*] The vice, therefore, which is in men, ought not to be imputed to Scripture.

### Objection XI. Not all things are written — **John 21:25**. *Ergo* controversies in matters of faith cannot be judged from Scripture alone.

**Respondeo**: **This objection was long ago solved by the Fathers themselves.** [*Luther: T. 1, p. 445*] Christ did many things which are not written. But those things were chosen which should be written (as **Augustine, *Tractate 49 In Ioannem***, says) *"which seemed to suffice for the salvation of believers."* And **Cyril, In Iohannem**: *"Not all things which the Lord did are written, but those which the writers thought sufficient both for morals and for dogmas."*

### Objection XII. Scripture contains many difficult and obscure things. *Ergo* in matters of faith it cannot be the supreme judge.

**Respondeo** with **Augustine**: *"Although many things in Scripture are difficult and obscure, in those which are manifestly placed are found all things which pertain to faith and to the manners of living."* Certain more difficult passages, therefore, do not impede the office of Scripture as supreme judge in controversies of religion — since nothing is drawn from the obscurities of Scripture which is not most plainly said elsewhere.

If they object that there are certain questions which cannot be sufficiently expedited in this life from Scripture, etc. — the response is plain: *some questions are to be committed to God* [*As if it were asked what God did before he made the world, and the like*]; let it suffice us that those things which are necessary to salvation are set forth sufficiently perspicuously in the Scriptures.

### Objection XIII. That thing cannot sustain the office of supreme judge in controversies of religion to which in disputes it is not lawful to appeal. But **Tertullian, *De Praescriptionibus***, witnesses that in disputations concerning religion it is not lawful to appeal to the Scriptures, nor is a contest to be constituted in these in which either there is no victory, or an uncertain one. *Ergo* it cannot sustain the office of supreme judge.

**Respondeo** to the minor: **It is a distortion and depravation of Tertullian's words.** For Tertullian disputes against heretics — the Valentinians and the like — who partly rejected the Scriptures and partly by additions and subtractions corrupted them. When one deals with such (says Tertullian), one is not to appeal to the Scriptures, nor is a contest to be constituted in these. But let us hear Tertullian himself speaking: *"This heresy does not receive certain Scriptures; and if it receives any, by additions and subtractions to the disposition of its own institute it perverts; and if it receives them, it does not receive them entire. If it holds them in some measure entire, nevertheless by diverse expositions commented upon it converts them."* Shortly after he concludes: *"Therefore one is not to appeal to the Scriptures, nor is a contest to be constituted in them"* — namely, when one has to deal with those heretics who either do not receive the Scriptures, or by additions and subtractions of their own pervert those which they do receive to the disposition of their own institute. [*The heretics of Tertullian's history*] For in vain against such heretics one appeals to the Scriptures, who either do not receive the Scriptures, or by their own additions and subtractions manifestly corrupt them.

Nevertheless, that they may not glory in the truth, Tertullian further admonishes that ***that doctrine alone is to be held for true which the Churches received from the Apostles, the Apostles from Christ, and Christ from God***. All remaining doctrines which savour against the truth of the Apostolic Churches and of Christ and of God are to be reputed as lying or false.

These things considered, it is plain: **Tertullian does not simply deny that Sacred Scripture is the supreme judge in controversies of religion**, but only when the dispute is with those who either wholly reject the Scriptures, or by additions and subtractions pervert them to the disposition of their own institute. The Papists therefore do injury to Tertullian, when they contend that his words, spoken *secundum quid*, ought to be received *simply*.

— ❦ —

## Question 19. What is a Christian man to think and to hold of Councils?

**What is a Christian man to think and to hold of Councils?**

**Let him hold them in the greatest esteem** [*Authority of general councils*] — yet with such condition, that he *build his faith on the Word of God alone*. For God alone has authority to establish articles of faith, and no man. But it is the office of Councils **not to pronounce or show forth anything except what is grounded and revealed in the Sacred Letters**, and to give the orthodox and Scripture-consonant explication of those sayings which are perversely and evilly cited by false doctors and heretics. [*Luther: T. 7, pp. 255, 256, 257 and following*] Whence Christ, **Luke 24:48**: *"But you are witnesses of these things."*

— ❦ —

## Question 20. Are we bound to believe the Councils even apart from a certain Word of God?

**Are we bound to believe the Councils even apart from a certain Word of God?**

**By no means.** For **Christ** says at **John 10:5, 27**: *"My sheep hear my voice; a stranger they will not follow."* And **Paul, Gal. 1:8**: *"But though we or an angel from heaven should teach any other Gospel, let him be anathema."*

— ❦ —

## Question 21. If, however, the Councils are to be believed only ***according to and up to*** the Word of God, what shall be the end of contentions and controversies?

**What shall be the end of contentions and controversies?**

**Respondeo**: **If the erring party will not suffer itself to be informed from the Word of God, but stubbornly persists in the conceived opinion, then God himself at last intervenes, terminates the strife, and decides the controversy** — as he dealt with **Arius** and other heretics. And there remains besides the ***Last Judgment***, in which all impiety shall pay its deserved penalties.

— ❦ —

## Question 22. What, then, is to be thought of **human traditions**?

**What is to be thought of human traditions?**

**The Papists attribute to them the same authority as to Sacred Scripture — but evilly and blasphemously.** [*Luther: T. 5, pp. 110, 117, 118*] For this without exception is most true: ***that which is made known by God himself, and handed to the Church, is both better and to be preferred to that which is invented by unclean men***.

And Christ himself expressly says, **Matt. 15:9**: *"In vain do they worship God, teaching doctrines and commandments of men."*

Besides, the Scripture bids ***"prove all things"*** — **1 Thess. 5:21** — and hold that which is *good*, that is, what is *analogous to the Word of God*. But the rule of proof, as has been said, is the ***Word of God***.

To this, Christ did not say: *"I am human tradition or custom"*, but ***"I am the Truth"***. And **Cyprian to Pompeian**: ***"Consuetudo sine veritate vetustas erroris est: propter quod, relicto errore, sequamur veritatem."*** — *"Custom without truth is the antiquity of error: wherefore, error being forsaken, let us follow the truth."*

— ❦ —

## Question 23. What is the distinction between the doctrine of the Heathen and of the Christian Church?

**What is the distinction between the doctrine of the Heathen and of the Christian Church?**

**I.** The doctrine of the Gentiles is ***a fabrication of human reason***; but Scripture is ***made known and handed down by God himself***.

**II.** The doctrine of the Gentiles embraces only a small particle of the Law, of the Law and of the external virtues. Of the essence and the most clement will of God toward sinful men it has nothing certain. But the doctrine of the Christian Church, both of the Law and of the Gospel, is ***entire, and sufficient and perfect for the true knowledge of God***.

**III.** The doctrine of the Gentiles ***introduces and establishes idolatry and the invocation of those who are not gods***; but Scripture hands down alone the knowledge and invocation of the ***only true God***.

**IV.** The doctrine of the Gentiles is not an instrument and means whereby God illuminates and converts the hearts of men, but is rather an organ whereby the Devil leads men away from God. But the doctrine of the Church, or the Word of God, is the means through which God ***regenerates, renews, illuminates the hearts of men, and sanctifies them unto eternal life***. **Rom. 1:16** — *"The Gospel is the power of God unto salvation to everyone that believeth."*

— ❦ —

```{=openxml}
<w:p><w:r><w:br w:type="page"/></w:r></w:p>
```

# Locus II. Of God

### *De Deo*

— ❦ —

## Question 1. How and whence does God rightly will to be known?

**How and whence does God rightly will to be known?**

**In and from his Word, in which he has made himself known.** [*Luther: T. 4, p. 390; T. 7, pp. 64, 65; T. 8, p. 288; T. 2, part 1, pp. 60, 61, 62; T. 4, part 1, p. 5; T. 4, part 1, p. 130*] **Ps. 119:105** — *"Thy word is a lamp unto my feet, and a light unto my paths."*

— ❦ —

## Question 2. What is God?

**What is God?**

**Let the simpler hold this description from the Catechism:** [*Luther: T. 5, pp. 199, 191; T. 7, p. 64; T. 2, p. 227; T. 4, part 1, p. 405, f. b.*]

**God is one in individual spiritual essence, three in Persons, who created all things and sustains them; who redeemed us — lost and damned men — through his Son, and sanctifies us through the Holy Spirit, calls us to his Church, illuminates us, remits sins, will raise us from the dead at the last day, and will give eternal life to all believers, but the impenitent impious he will cast off into eternal fire.**

This description, as easily may be seen, is drawn from the Word of God and from the Apostles' Creed.

**John 4:24** — *"God is a Spirit."* **Matt. 28:19** — *"Going, teach ye all nations, baptising them in the name of the Father, and of the Son, and of the Holy Spirit."* **Gen. 1:1** — *"In the beginning God created the heaven and the earth."* **John 3:16** — *"For so God loved the world, as to give his only-begotten Son, that whosoever believeth in him may not perish, but may have life everlasting."* **John 5:28–29** — *"Wonder not at this, for the hour cometh, wherein all that are in the graves shall hear the voice of the Son of God, and shall come forth: they that have done good things unto the resurrection of life, but they that have done evil unto the resurrection of judgement."*

— ❦ —

## Question 3. What are the chief idioms and attributes proper to God?

**What are the chief idioms and attributes proper to God?**

**These.**

**I.** That he is ***eternal*** — **Gen. 21:33**: *"He called upon the name of the Lord, the Everlasting God."*

**II.** That he is ***omnipotent and all-sufficient***, and consequently does whatsoever he wills, and can deliver from all evils. [*Luther: T. 1, p. 477; T. 3, p. 351; Gen. T. 2, p. 187; Gen. T. 3, p. 149*] **Gen. 17:1** — *"I am the Almighty Lord — El-Schaddai."*

**III.** That he is ***truthful***, and wonderfully keeps and performs what he has promised. [*Luther: T. 4, p. 449; T. 7, p. 150*] **John 4; Ps. 33:4** — *"For the word of the Lord is right, and all his works are done with faithfulness."*

**IV.** That he is ***just and supreme Judge***, and therefore also to be feared. [*Luther: T. 1, p. 31*] **Ps. 7:12–13** — *"God is a just Judge, strong and patient — is he angry every day? Except you will be converted, he has shaken his sword; he has bent his bow, and made it ready. And in it he has prepared the instruments of death, he has made his arrows for the burning."*

**V.** That he is ***long-suffering, most clement, and most merciful***; wherefore they who do repentance ought not to doubt of his mercy and goodness. [*Luther: T. 4, p. 102, from the Sermon at Pentecost*] **Ps. 103:8–12** — *"The Lord is compassionate and merciful; long-suffering and plenteous in mercy... As a father hath compassion on his children, so hath the Lord compassion on them that fear him."* **John 3:16**.

— ❦ —

## Question 4. Are the properties of God — for example, wisdom, omnipotence, justice, and the like — ***accidents***?

**Are the properties of God accidents?**

**By no means.** For if they were accidents, as they are in us, they would be in him ***from elsewhere and mutably***. But that this is most false is clear to all. For as God has not his origin from elsewhere, so neither are the properties in him ***accidental*** — as we see the virtues in angels and men to be accidents. Hence it is rightly said: ***to the Divinity, to live is not one thing and to know another; nor is it another thing in God to be a living wisdom than to be a wise life***. For God is not mutable — **Mal. 3:6**: *"I am God, and I change not."* **Ps. 102:27** — *"But thou art the selfsame."* And accidents are ***in creatures, not in the Creator***.

### Objection. Every ***relation*** is an accident. In God there are relations. *Ergo* in God there are accidents.

**Respondeo**: **The major is true of the relations of creatures. But in the divine essence, by no means.** For it is agreed that the relations in the persons of the Divinity ***in no way really differ from the essence itself of any Person***. Nor are essence and relation two things in God; for the essence of the Father and the relation of the Father is the Father himself — one and the same in the person of the Father. So also of the remaining Persons.

— ❦ —

## Question 5. Is there therefore only one God in essence — or only one divine essence?

**Is there only one God in essence, or only one divine essence?**

**There is one in essence and nature, eternal, and true God.** **Deut. 6:4** — *"Hear, O Israel: The Lord our God is one Lord."* **Isa. 44:6** — *"Thus saith the Lord of Israel, and his redeemer the Lord of hosts: I am the first, and I am the last, and besides me there is no God."* And **v. 8** — *"You are my witnesses. Is there a God besides me, or a maker whom I have not known?"*

### Objection I. One God could not suffice to govern and preserve all creatures. *Ergo* there are many Gods.

**Respondeo**: **He can suffice for all, since he is omnipotent.** **Gen. 17:1** — *"I am the Lord Almighty — El-Schaddai"* — signifies such a Lord as is sufficient for all creatures, for nourishing, governing, and preserving them.

### Objection II. If God were one, he would also govern in one manner, and would do well to the good and ill to the evil. But we see the contrary happen. *Ergo* [there are more Gods].

**Respondeo** to the Major: **God has his own causes why in this life he sometimes spares the evil and burdens the good with the cross and calamities.** He tolerates the evil patiently for a time, that by his long-suffering he may call them to repentance — **Rom. 2:4**. The good, however, he presses with the cross that he may exercise their faith, hope, and patience. Whence **Ps. 119:71** says: *"It is good for me, Lord, that thou hast humbled me, that I may learn thy justifications."*

— ❦ —

## Question 6. Whence is the vocable ***essentia*** derived?

**Whence is the vocable "essence" derived?**

**From Exodus 3:14**, where God says to Moses: **"I am who am."** [*Luther: T. 4, part 1, p. 118; T. 4, part 1, p. 131*] For because he exists from himself, and has his beginning from nowhere else, but all creatures were made by him — he rightly claims this appellation for himself. Likewise from **Rev. 3** [i.e. Rev. 1:8]: *"who is, who was, who is to come."*

The vocable ***essentia*** in this article is opposed:

**First**, to all fictitious things — for example, to the ***idols of the Gentiles***, which are not from themselves, but have their being from elsewhere.

**Next**, it shows the vast difference between the essence of God and the essence of angels and men. For the divine essence is ***uncreated, eternal, and infinite***; but angels and men are of ***created and finite essence***.

— ❦ —

## Question 7. What, then, does ***essentia*** signify?

**What does essentia signify?**

***οὐσία*** or ***essentia*** signifies the **community of nature**, or that which pertains simply to God, and therefore is predicated in common of all the Persons.

The ***community or common in God*** is called that which pertains not only to the Father, but also to the Son and to the Holy Spirit. As, to be eternal, wise, just, etc. — is proper to all three Persons. For there is **one essence of all three Persons of the Divinity**.

— ❦ —

## Question 8. How is the word ***essentia*** considered in this article?

**How is the word essentia considered in this article?**

**In two ways: ***absolutely*** and ***relatively***.**

**Absolutely** it is said to be considered when it is taken of the whole Divinity, or of all three Persons.

**Relatively**, when it designates a *hypostasis or Person*, according to the property by which each is discerned from the remaining Persons — as when it is said of the person of the ***Father*** that he ***begets*** the Son; of the person of the ***Son*** that he ***is begotten*** by the Father; of the person of the ***Holy Spirit*** that he ***proceeds from the Father and the Son***.

— ❦ —

## Question 9. Why then does Scripture use the vocable ***Elohim***, which is of plural number — if there is only one essence of the Divinity, and not more?

**Why does Scripture use the plural word Elohim?**

**Respondeo**: **She uses this vocable, not because she teaches a plurality of Gods, but because there are more Persons in the Divinity.** For one is the Person of the Father, another of the Son, another of the Holy Spirit.

— ❦ —

## Question 10. *Ergo* Scripture teaches that there are ***more Persons*** in the Divinity?

**Does Scripture teach that there are more Persons in the Divinity?**

**Yes, she does teach it.** And of this assertion these are the ***infallible documents***:

**I.** That by the ***names of Father, Son, and Holy Spirit***, she plainly indicates the distinction of Persons.

**II.** That she has ***perspicuous testimonies*** in which the Father is discerned from the Son. **Ps. 2:7** — *"This day have I begotten thee."* **Gal. 4:4** — *"God sent his Son."*

**III.** That she supplies sayings in which the Son discerns his own Person from the Person of the Father — as **John 8:14–18**: *"My testimony is true; for I am not alone, but I, and the Father that sent me. I am one that give testimony of myself: and the Father that sent me giveth testimony of me."*

**IV.** That similarly she discerns the ***Holy Spirit*** in manifest words from the Father and the Son. **John 14:16** — *"I will ask the Father, and he shall give you another Paraclete."*

**V.** That even in the ***Old Testament***, she everywhere brings forward testimonies of the Trinity of Persons. [*Genesis T. 3, p. 194*] **Gen. 1** — *"In the beginning ***Elohim*** created the heaven and the earth."* And **Ps. 33**.

— ❦ —

## Question 11. Whence is the vocable ***Trinity*** in this mystery derived?

**Whence is the vocable Trinity in this mystery derived?**

**Respondeo**: **Without doubt from the saying at 1 John 5:7**, where it is said: ***"There are three who give testimony in heaven: the Father, the Word, and the Holy Spirit — and these three are one."***

— ❦ —

## Question 12. Is it lawful also to say that there are ***three Gods***, just as we say that there are three Persons of the Divinity?

**Is it lawful to say there are three Gods?**

**Respondeo**: **It is not lawful to say three Gods.** For our faith admits no number of Gods.

— ❦ —

## Question 13. How many Persons, then, are in this divine essence?

**How many Persons in this divine essence?**

**There are three distinct Persons — Father, Son, and Holy Spirit; and these three Persons are one, true, and eternal God in essence and nature, Creator of heaven and earth, and of all things which are in them.** [*Luther: T. 6, pp. 544, 545; T. 4, part 1, pp. 786, 787*] Which against the **Arians, Macedonians, Sabellians**, and similar heretics is to be held with firm faith. The testimonies of Scripture have just before been cited.

— ❦ —

## Question 14. Do the ***Jews, Turks***, and others act rightly, who reject the doctrine and confession of the sacrosanct Trinity?

**Do the Jews, Turks, and others rightly reject the doctrine of the Trinity?**

**By no means.** For since ***no one has ever seen God, nor even can see him***, we ought not to conclude according to the blindness of the judgement of our reason: *"The divine essence is one and individual, therefore there is also one Person of that essence, or in that essence."* For of God we ought not to think or believe otherwise than as he has made himself known in his Word. Since therefore we have express testimonies of the Word of God concerning the Trinity of Persons in one individual essence of the Divinity, let us ***follow Scripture as the testimony of God himself concerning himself***, and set aside the judgement or consequences of our reason.

### Objection I. That which is one in number cannot be divided by numbers. But the true God is one in number. *Ergo* he cannot be divided by numbers.

**Respondeo**: **I concede the whole argument, if thou speakest of the essence of the Divinity.** It is another matter, however, when the discourse is of the Persons. According to essence, God neither can nor ought to be numbered. For he is one only in essence; but the Persons of the Divinity are numbered as three. The reason is, ***that God has thus and not otherwise made himself known***.

### Objection II. Wheresoever there are three substantial Persons, there are three separated substances. But you number three Persons. *Ergo* it would follow that there are also three distinct Gods.

**Respondeo**: **It is different to number Persons of the Divinity, and to say that there are three essences or separated substances, or consequently three Gods in number.** For we do not number essences, nor do we say that the essence of the Divinity is separated or divided. For it is always and remains **one and indivisible**. But we number the Persons of the Divinity — and that according to the manifestation. **Matt. 3:17; John 1**.

### Objection III. Three things and one thing are four. But three Persons and one essence are taught. *Ergo* a ***quaternity*** is taught.

**Respondeo**: **It is the fallacy of composition and division.** For the essence of God is not outside the Trinity of Persons, and something distinct and separated from the three Persons. But those three Persons — Father, Son, and Holy Spirit — are that one and true God, or that one divine essence; and in that one essence are those three Persons; which Persons in essence are one true and eternal God, ***not three Gods or three essences in number***. Three Persons are in number, but not three essences in number. Simply, within the bounds of manifestation in this arcane mystery, we ought to stand.

— ❦ —

## Question 15. Are the Persons of the Divinity, then, ***really and truly*** distinguished among themselves?

**Are the Persons of the Divinity really and truly distinguished among themselves?**

**Most greatly.** For Scripture openly says that ***there are three who give testimony***; nor can it be that the same is at once the ***sender and the sent***. But the Father sent the Son into the world — **John 3:34**. Absurd also would be the invocation of the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit in Baptism, if the Persons of the Divinity were not really distinguished among themselves. Finally, there must be a difference between him who speaks and those with whom he speaks. But the Father, at **Gen. 1:26**, says: *"Let us make man to our own image."* There is therefore a difference between the ***Father speaking***, and the remaining Persons of the Divinity — namely, ***the Son and the Holy Spirit***, whom he addresses. The same difference the ***peculiar manifestations*** also demonstrate, as in the Baptism of Christ, **Matt. 3, John 1, and Matt. 17**. From which it is manifest that the ***Sabellians*** and their like grievously err, who altogether abolished the real distinction of the Persons of the Divinity.

### Objection I. God is one — **Deut. 6**. *Ergo* also only one Person of the Divinity.

**Respondeo**: **He is one in essence** — for there is only one essence of the Divinity, and not more. But in that one and most simple essence are three Persons. For thus has God made himself known.

### Objection II. He who says there are more Persons in the Divinity introduces a plurality of Gods. You say there are more Persons in the Divinity. *Ergo* you introduce a plurality of Gods.

**Respondeo**: **The major is false.** For Scripture, which teaches that there is only one essence of the Divinity, shows at the same time that in that one essence there are three distinct Persons — and yet does not introduce a plurality of Gods. For these three Persons are not three Gods, but that one true and eternal God.

### Objection III. The Trinity is distinguished not by Persons, but by the names of ***offices***. In heaven he is called ***Father***; on earth, ***Son***; in the powers of creatures, ***Holy Spirit***. *Ergo* there is only one Person of the Deity.

**Respondeo**: **It is false that the Trinity is distinguished only by names of offices.** For Scripture openly names three Persons — **Father**, ***λόγον***, and **Holy Spirit**. **1 John 5:7** — *"Three there are who bear witness in heaven — the Father, the Word, and the Holy Spirit; and these three are one."* And by name she distinguishes between the Father and the Son. **John 3:16** — *"So God loved the world, that he gave his only-begotten Son."* And **Gal. 4:4** — *"When the fulness of the time was come, God sent his Son,"* etc.

### Objection IV. If the Persons of the Divinity are really distinguished, they will not be one in essence, and the Trinity will not be unity.

**Respondeo**: **Human reason indeed cannot attain that the Persons of the Divinity are really distinct, and yet in essence be one; but Faith, according to the Word of God, believes this, and leads the reason captive.** For so the Lord himself in the sacred letters speaks concerning this mystery. [*Genesis T. 1, p. 3*] **Gen. 1** describes the creation of all things, and it is said that ***Elohim*** created the heaven and the earth. The verb ***Bara*** is of singular number, that the ***unity of essence*** may be expressed. The name ***Elohim*** is plural, that the ***trinity of Persons in that most simple unity*** may be denoted. This same is repeated **Gen. 2**: *"In the day when Jehovah Elohim made the heaven and the earth."* **Josh. 24:19** — *"Ye cannot serve the Lord, for Elohim (God) is holy himself."* Here expressly the unity is confirmed, and yet at the same time the plurality of Persons is asserted. For he calls the one Jehovah ***holy Gods*** — that is, three Persons, Father, Son, and Holy Spirit.

**Isa. 6:8** — *"Whom shall I send, or who shall go (in Hebrew ***Lanu***, ***nobis***) for us?"* And of this testimony **Jerome** on the 6th chapter of Isaiah writes that it indicates the Sacrament of the ***unity and Trinity***.

**Malachi 1:6** — *"If I am Lord (***Adonim***), where is my fear?"* God, as one Lord in essence, wills to be feared; and yet he speaks in the plural — ***Adonim***, ***Lords*** — that he may teach that in that one and most simple essence of the Divinity there are three distinct Persons.

In the words of Baptism (as **Ambrose, *De fide* lib. 1, cap. 1**, also observed) an illustrious testimony stands, that the three Persons of the Divinity are one in essence, and that truly there is a Trinity of Persons in the unity of the divine essence. Christ said: *"Baptise them in the name"* — in the ***name*** indeed, ***not in the names***. He himself also says: *"I and the Father are one."* He said ***one***, lest a division of power be made. And in the same place, cap. 2, the Lord, saying *"Go, baptise the nations in the name of the Father and of the Son and of the Holy Spirit,"* shows that the power of the Trinity is one.

— ❦ —

## Question 16. What, then, are the ***personal properties*** by which these three Persons of the Divinity are discerned among themselves?

**What are the personal properties by which the three Persons are distinguished?**

**The personal property of the Father** is to be of none, but from eternity to have begotten to himself the Son, ***ὁμοούσιον καὶ συναΐδιον*** — consubstantial and coeternal. [*Luther: T. 8, p. 153; T. 6, p. 542; T. 1, part 1, p. 535; T. 5, last-words of David*]

**The personal property of the Son** is to be from eternity begotten by the Father, ***ὁμοούσιον καὶ συναΐδιον*** — **Ps. 2:7**.

**The personal property of the Holy Spirit**, by which he is discerned from the Father and the Son, is to proceed from the Father and the Son — **John 15:26**.

Besides this ***internal*** distinction of the Persons, there is also ***another*** which is taken from the ***external manifestation***. The Father made himself known in the voice calling from heaven: *"This is my beloved Son, in whom I am well pleased."* The Son manifested himself in the flesh — in human nature — for only the Son was made man. The Holy Spirit alone was manifested in the form of a dove, in cloven tongues as of fire, in various tongues and miracles. Yet the human nature of Christ was formed by all three Persons of the Divinity; yet also the cloven tongues, and the gifts of the Holy Spirit, are the work of all three Persons.

— ❦ —

## Question 17. Recite to me briefly the sum of this venerable mystery.

**Recite briefly the sum of this mystery.**

**The sum of this stupendous mystery thou hast briefly comprised in the Symbol of Athanasius:** [*Luther: T. 3, p. 509; T. 8, p. 154; T. 1, part 1, pp. 532, 533*]

*"One is the Person of the Father, another of the Son, another of the Holy Spirit; but of the Father and of the Son and of the Holy Spirit there is one divinity, equal glory, coeternal majesty,"* etc. The Father is made by none, nor begotten; the Son is of the Father alone, not made, nor created, but begotten; the Holy Spirit is of the Father and of the Son, not made, nor created, nor begotten, but proceeding. One therefore Father, not three Fathers; one Son, not three Sons; one Holy Spirit, not three Holy Spirits. And in this Trinity nothing is prior or posterior, nothing greater or lesser; but the whole three Persons are coeternal to themselves, and coequal.

Although therefore in the Sacred Page and History sometimes only one Person is addressed or appears — whether it be that of the Father, of the Son, or of the Holy Spirit, which manifests itself — yet the remaining two are not excluded. For with that Person and in that Person is present and is addressed **the whole, true, and eternal God, the whole sacrosanct Trinity — Father, Son, and Holy Spirit**. [*Luther: T. 8, p. 164*] Which is diligently to be noted, that we may rightly recognise the divine majesty, and lest with the **Jews, Mahometans**, and other blasphemous heretics we believe only one Person of the Divinity and deny the Divinity of the remaining two. For not thus — but as he made himself known in his Word, and chiefly in and with his Son, and through his Son, will God be recognised. Wherefore also through the whole of the Old and New Testament writings he bears testimony to us of his Son, and sets him forth for us to recognise and to worship. **John 5:39** — *"Search the Scriptures"*, says Christ; *"they are they that give testimony of me."*

— ❦ —

## Question 18. Can a man be saved without the knowledge of this article?

**Can a man be saved without the knowledge of this article?**

**By no means.** [*Luther: T. 8, p. 271*] For let no one presume that he can enter the kingdom of heaven without knowledge of this article, that there are three distinct Persons of the Divinity — Father, Son, and Holy Spirit — and yet these three Persons are one individual and eternal essence. For thus God wills to be known; and this is our Christian Faith, into which we are baptised and called — **Matt. 28; Mark 16**. And *"whoever wishes to be saved, it is necessary that he hold the Catholic Faith; which unless every one shall preserve entire and inviolate, without doubt he shall perish eternally. But the Catholic Faith is this, that we worship one God in Trinity, and Trinity in Unity"* — as the Symbol of Athanasius has.

— ❦ —

## Question 19. Instruct me briefly of the Person of the ***Father***.

**Instruct me of the Person of the Father.**

**The Father is the first Person of the sacrosanct Trinity, from whom the Son is begotten from eternity, from whom (and from the Son) the Holy Spirit proceeds** — so that in respect of order the Father may rightly be called as it were the ***fountain and origin*** of the Divinity. [*Luther: T. 5, p. 156*]

And when the Father is named, the Son is not excluded, but is included and co-named. And so also the Holy Spirit is together addressed and included when the Father or the Son is named — since ***no Person is or can be God separately outside another***.

So **St. Paul** and **Peter** speak: *"Blessed be the God and Father of our Lord Jesus Christ, the Father of all mercies and grace,"* etc. And **Christ** himself, in the Evangelical History, always sets the Father before, and ascribes all to him — and yet at **John 5:23** says: *"That all men may honour the Son, as they honour the Father."* And **John 16:15** — *"All things whatsoever the Father hath, are mine,"* etc. — save that the Father is the first Person, from whom the Son from eternity in generation has received all things; and not, on the contrary, the Father from the Son. That certain works, however, are ***distinctly*** said to be and to be done ***to the Father or to the Son or to the Holy Spirit*** — this is to be referred ***to the manifestation, not to the separation, of the Persons***.

— ❦ —

## Question 20. What does the vocable ***persona*** signify?

**What does the word "person" signify?**

**Respondeo**: **In this article it signifies an individual substance, intelligent, incommunicable, not sustained in another nature.** To the Greeks the same is ***hypostasis*** — ***συρισάμενος***, something truly subsisting and not vanishing away — from the verb ***ὑφίστημι***, *to subsist*. It is distinguished from ***οὐσία***, as something in reality distinct from the other subsisting things.

— ❦ —

## Question 21. Whence is the word ***Persona*** derived?

**Whence is the word Persona derived?**
